### **@royaltyblessed2454** | 👍 171 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jIS2eB-rGv0&lc=Ugybc_CRU84fVhR721t4AaABAg)

Really appreciate the attempt to frame this through game theory and structural geopolitics rather than just headline reactions. The asymmetric warfare breakdown, Strait of Hormuz stakes, and GCC vulnerabilities around desalination and food imports are exactly the kind of context most coverage skips. The point about decapitation strikes potentially strengthening ideological resolve rather than collapsing regimes is also well made.

That said, the analysis would hit harder with more discipline around what’s confirmed, what’s inference, and what’s speculation. Conclusions like “Dubai is dead” or the American empire collapsing are presented as certainties when framed as probabilistic scenarios they’d actually be more persuasive as there’s a meaningful difference between “can be hit” and “will collapse.” The game theory framing also promises more than it delivers; even one actual payoff matrix would go a long way. Doesn’t take away from the fact this is a valuable watch, just think the substance here deserves the rigour to match it.

An issue in this video is the idea that the USA uses THAAD to intercept the drones. That idea is simply false. THAAD is used for interception of ballistic missiles and engages targets at ranges up to 200 kilometers. It cannot intercept Shahed type drones as they fly too slow and too low for it to engage and it also just does not make sense. THAAD usually intercepts in the higher atmosphere or a bit above. In addition to that, a THAAD interceptor costs about 12 million dollars not just 1 million. It wouldnt intercept 50.000 dollar drones.

> For example, divisions like the Roman Catholic Church and the Eastern Orthodox Church still believe in the same God and follow Jesus Christ. The disagreement is about authority, doctrine, and historical developments, not about a different deity.  
> Similarly, the Sunni–Shia difference in Islam historically came from a political question of leadership after the Prophet Muhammad’s death especially regarding Ali ibn Abi Talib. ### **@pmswanepoel** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jIS2eB-rGv0&lc=UgxsQqUhNzalxhghP5N4AaABAg)

I actually know about this guy and have watched quite a bit of his stuff. The overall argument still doesn’t really hold up to me, even though — like him — I’m not a Middle East expert (he certainly is convincing though!). Some of the points about drones and energy infrastructure are real issues analysts talk about, but he then builds a long chain of assumptions — Iran cripples the Gulf, Gulf investment stops, the AI bubble bursts, the US economy collapses. That’s a huge claim given the size of the U.S. economy. For that conclusion to work, all of those things would have to happen in sequence, and that kind of domino argument always makes me a bit sceptical. Whenever an argument jumps from a few real points to ‘the empire collapses’. I tend to slow down and look carefully at the steps in between. He mixes some genuine points with a lot of speculation, which makes the whole thing sound more convincing than it really is. It’s also worth noting he isn’t actually a Middle East or military specialist — his background is mainly education commentary and big-picture geopolitics. That doesn’t mean he must be wrong of course, but when you look closely a lot of the steps in the argument seem pretty shaky.

Professor,

I have watched many of your videos and I truly think your content is powerful. But I hope you are open to criticism, because I need to say this from my perspective.

I am Iranian, and what I am about to say is not from media — it is from real life.

As a professor of geopolitics, you have a responsibility to remain objective and not lean into ideological or one-sided narratives. But in your videos, there is a clear and repeated bias, especially when it comes to Trump and the United States.

Why don’t you talk about the fact that for more than 47 years, Iranian people have been under extreme pressure by their own government?

Why don’t you mention the kings, the repression, and the fear people live with daily?

Why don’t you talk about the protests in recent years, where thousands of young people — many in their 20s and 30s — were affected, dd, or disappeared?

Why don’t you mention cases where even people who tried to help others, like medical staff, were punished severely?

Why don’t you talk about how resources that belong to the people are used for military expansion, missiles, and regional influence instead of improving lives?

Why don’t you mention the internet restrictions — even now, when I am writing this, millions of people struggle with access to the global internet?

You talk about neutrality, but neutrality is not ignoring reality. Presenting a system as “neutral” while avoiding these facts is not neutrality — it is selective framing.

If you are truly a geopolitics professor, then your analysis should include all sides — including the lived reality of millions of people inside countries like Iran.

Also, when discussing major events, why don’t you address cases like the Ukrainian passenger plane incident (Flight PS752), which was later explained as “human error”?

I am not speaking emotionally. I am speaking as someone who has seen things with his own eyes.

When you have a platform, you have responsibility. And when you choose what to include and what to ignore, that shapes how millions of people understand the world.

This is why I am asking you — respectfully but directly — to be truly balanced, not selectively neutral.

Sincerely,  
An Iran

### **@high-reshallucinations** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jIS2eB-rGv0&lc=UgxCHVxTUmfcX0PYcT94AaABAg)

the war is real and ongoing as of March 17, 2026; Reuters and AP both describe an ongoing U.S.-Israeli war with Iran, now in its third week, with casualties across the region and major disruption around the Strait of Hormuz.

the Strait of Hormuz is genuinely central to the crisis; Reuters notes it handles about 20% of global oil and LNG trade, and officials are warning that escorts alone cannot guarantee safe passage.

Gulf states and infrastructure are exposed to Iranian strikes; Reuters reports attacks and injuries across Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, the UAE, Jordan, Bahrain, Iraq, and Israel.

But I do not agree with the video’s broader framing.

What I think is overstated or unreliable:

calling this “World War II” is plainly rhetorical; current reporting shows a severe regional war with global economic consequences, not a verified world war involving all major powers.

“Dubai is dead” and “the entire GCC will collapse” are dramatic predictions, not established facts. There are strikes and panic effects, but that is very different from proven irreversible collapse.

“Europe will enter,” “Russia and China will also enter,” and similar inevitability claims are not supported by what’s publicly reported. In fact, the EU is publicly pushing diplomacy, and Poland has explicitly said it will not send troops.

a lot of the motive-claims are asserted far beyond the evidence, especially the parts about a grand plan to fragment Iran, or that the U.S. military “doesn’t really do anything.” Those are ideological conclusions, not demonstrated facts.

So my verdict is:

Agree with the strategic warning; disagree with the certainty and the grand theory.

He is strongest when saying:

Hormuz matters

Gulf infrastructure is vulnerable

escalation can spiral

a ground war against Iran would be dangerous

He is weakest when saying:

he knows the hidden master plan

collapse outcomes are inevitable

every actor’s motives are simple and certain

The useful way to watch him is as high-drama scenario analysis, not as a source of verified truth.

Professor Jang's framework, for all its sophistication, makes the same error as every empire in history: it mistakes the map for the territory, the plan for the reality, the elite for the whole.

The planners plan. But the people resist—not always, not everywhere, not effectively every time, but persistently enough that no empire has ever achieved permanent dominance. The Roman Empire fell. The British Empire fell. The Soviet Empire fell. The American Empire, if it is falling, will fall not because the planners planned it, but because people stopped being fuel for it.

This is the counter-thesis: The tenacity of ordinary humans to refuse, resist, and rebuild has always been the empire's unsolvable problem.

Interesting analysis, but the argument seems to lean a bit too heavily on theoretical game theory. In reality, the geopolitical landscape is far more complex and doesn’t necessarily follow a neat script. The assumption that the US would be drawn into a long conflict on Iran’s terms overlooks the multitude of strategic choices available. Plus, it also seems to rely on the idea that Russia and China would step in, which so far looks quite unlikely. So while it’s a thought provoking scenario, it feels a bit too reductive for real world policymaking.

### **@ramkumarrealm**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jIS2eB-rGv0&lc=UgwdI8DbA6kzYXqkrGx4AaABAg)

Core setup of the war

The war begins with a US‑Israeli “decapitation strike” that kills Iran’s Supreme Leader and many family members, plus a strike that hits a girls’ school; inside Iran this is framed as martyrdom and jihad, not just politics.

​



Because Shia Islam sacralizes martyrdom, the professor says Iranians interpret the leader’s death as a religious duty to fight “to the death” against the “Great Satan,” making this a jihad rather than a limited, negotiable conflict.

​



Iran responds by declaring total war and decentralizing command, so killing leaders in Tehran does not stop operations elsewhere.

​



GCC, Strait of Hormuz, and global economy

The professor calls the Strait of Hormuz the “pivot of the world,” noting that around 20% of global oil passes through it, with countries like India, China, and Japan hugely dependent; closure threatens worldwide recession or depression.

​



GCC states (Dubai, Abu Dhabi, Qatar, Bahrain, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia) are rich but extremely fragile: they import about 80% of their food and get roughly 60% of their water from easily targeted desalination plants.

​



Iran, a “mountain fortress,” can hide missiles and drones in its terrain and strike oil fields, desal plants, ports, and US bases across the flat, exposed GCC, which he argues they cannot realistically defend.

​



Asymmetry, drones, and US military doctrine

Iran uses cheap Shahed‑type drones (about 35–50k USD each, mass‑produced in the tens of thousands) to attack critical infrastructure.

​



The US responds with very expensive systems like THAAD and Patriot, where each interceptor missile can cost around 1 million USD and batteries are large, static, and vulnerable, creating a massive cost and mobility disadvantage.

​



He claims US military doctrine and procurement are stuck in Cold War “show the flag” thinking and riddled with corruption, building impressive but inefficient systems designed more to spend money than to win asymmetric wars.

​



Water, internal fractures, and US‑Israeli strategy

Both Iran and GCC face severe water stress, but GCC numbers are extreme (he cites stress levels in the hundreds or thousands of percent for Egypt, Saudi Arabia, Bahrain, Dubai), making destruction of desalination plants a near‑instant kill switch for those states.

​



Iran also has serious water problems and a very diverse ethnic map; the lecture says the US‑Israeli “optimal” long‑term game‑theory strategy is to bomb water infrastructure and encourage ethnic fragmentation, turning Iran into weak, competing mini‑states fighting over water.

​



This plan is described as morally “evil” and therefore never openly admitted, but inferred from geography, water, and ethnic distribution.

​



Iran’s counter‑strategy and world war risk

Iran’s counter‑strategy is to globalize the conflict into a Shia‑led jihad and broader Islamic revolution against pro‑US dictatorships, aiming for a “Pax Islamica” where a unified Muslim world ends American dominance.

​



He notes Shia populations across Pakistan, Iraq, the Gulf, etc., and mentions early attacks or protests around US embassies as signs of this emerging transnational jihad.

​



Because Europe depends on GCC energy (especially after reducing Russian imports), he argues Germany, France, and Britain may be pulled in on the US side, while Russia cannot allow Iran to fall and China may play a more ambivalent role; this web of interests makes the conflict akin to a new world war.

​

I’m an Iranian living in Tehran. The video was great, but a few key assumptions about Iran seem off.

First, public dissatisfaction with the government is underestimated. The ideology of martyrdom has lost much of its appeal, and resentment toward the regime and political Islam is far stronger than many assume. The government’s biggest fear today is another nationwide protest movement.

Second, Iranian society differs from places like Arab countries, Pakistan, or Afghanistan in how central religion is. The idea of jihad has little appeal among the general population, and even many regime supporters are motivated more by economic interests than ideology.

Third, ethnic divisions in Iran are often overstated. Most ethnic groups strongly identify with Iranian culture and generally feel closer to Iran than to neighboring countries.

Finally, Iran’s economy and regional influence have weakened in recent years, while public frustration has reached a critical level, as shown by the last protest wave that spread across cities throughout the country. The regime had to kill more than 30000 people to suppress it

I realize your popularity is rising and people invest your views with credibility,  but the American empire is broader than the petrodollar. It includes the treasury market, and it's unclear how this war will impact the safe harbor of US bonds, or the bond market. 

Several additional structures reinforce the dollar:

*Global derivatives market
*Dollar clearing banks in NY
*SWIFT messaging
*US military protection of shipping lanes
*Even US capital markets

Even if crisis starts in or by the US, money still runs into US assets. If the GCC collapses, oil markets will still function, and it would not immediately wreck the dollar system. Oil markets are 3-4 trillion annually; financial markets are hundreds of trillions. Only a credible alternative reserve option would ruin the dollar, and no replacement exists yet. The key is faith in treasury bonds.

Edit: I wouldn't necessarily be sad to see the AI industry hindered, as it threatens civil rights and liberties everywhere. Also, it's interesting why global money keeps flowing to the US, but it does. The empire will have to fall because of a military defeat.
